=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
There are N cells numbered from 1 to N in a row. Initially, M cells contain stones, and cell X_i contains A_i stones (1 \leq i \leq M).
You can perform the following operation any number of times (possibly zero):

- If cell i (1 \leq i \leq N-1) contains a stone, move one stone from cell i to cell i+1.

Find the minimum number of operations required to reach a state where each of the N cells contains exactly one stone. If it is impossible, print -1.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N M
X_1 X_2 \ldots X_M
A_1 A_2 \ldots A_M

Output

Print the answer.

Constraints


- 2 \leq N \leq 2 \times 10^{9}
- 1 \leq M \leq 2 \times 10^{5}
- M \leq N
- 1 \leq X_i \leq N (1 \leq i \leq M)
- X_i \neq X_j (1 \leq i < j \leq M)
- 1 \leq A_i \leq 2 \times 10^{9} (1 \leq i \leq M)
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

5 2
1 4
3 2

Sample Output 1

4

You can reach a state where each of the five cells contains exactly one stone with four operations as follows:

- Move one stone from cell 1 to cell 2.
- Move one stone from cell 2 to cell 3.
- Move one stone from cell 4 to cell 5.
- Move one stone from cell 1 to cell 2.

It is impossible to achieve the goal in three or fewer operations. Therefore, print 4.

Sample Input 2

10 3
1 4 8
4 2 4

Sample Output 2

-1

No matter how you perform the operations, you cannot reach a state where all ten cells contain exactly one stone. Therefore, print -1.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Greedy moves surplus right; total operations equals sum surplus moved? Actually each surplus stone may move multiple cells; total operations = sum over edges number of stones crossing edge. Number crossing edge k->k+1 = S(k)-k (if nonnegative). Since S(k)>=k, total operations = Σ_{k=1}^{N-1} (S(k)-k). Is this equal to formula? Let's check: Σ_{k=1}^{N-1} (S(k)-k) = Σ_{k=1}^{N-1} S(k) - N(N-1)/2. Is this equal to N(N+1)/2 - Σ sources? Let's derive identity: Σ_{k=1}^{N-1} S(k) = Σ_{stones} (# of prefixes k >= source and k<=N-1) = Σ_{stones} (N - source) for source<=N? For each stone at source s, it contributes to S(k) for k=s..N-1, count N-s. Then total ops = Σ(N-s) - N(N-1)/2 = N^2 - Σs - (N^2-N)/2 = N(N+1)/2 - Σs. Yes. Good. Greedy achieves formula. This is another proof. It also shows if prefix condition holds, sequence exists.

Actually, the operation count in greedy: At each cell k, after ensuring one stone remains, move all surplus stones one step to k+1. But if a surplus stone later moves further, counted each edge. Total operations = Σ surplus crossing edges. This equals formula. Nice.

Let's produce final code:
import sys
def main():
    data=list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data: return
    N=data[0]; M=data[1]
    xs=data[2:2+M]
    As=data[2+M:2+2*M]
    if sum(As) != N:
        print(-1); return
    pairs=sorted(zip(xs, As))
    cum=0
    for x,a in pairs:
        if cum < x - 1:
            print(-1); return
        cum += a
    # after loop cum should N
    # if cum != N? already sum=N
    total_targets = N*(N+1)//2
    source_sum = sum(x*a for x,a in pairs) # or sum(xs[i]*As[i])
    ans = total_targets - source_sum
    print(ans)
if __name__ == "__main__": main()

Let's write code:
import sys

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    N, M = data[0], data[1]
    X = data[2:2+M]
    A = data[2+M:2+2*M]

    if sum(A) != N:
        print(-1)
        return

    pairs = sorted(zip(X, A))
    cum = 0
    source_sum = 0
    for x, a in pairs:
        if cum < x - 1:
            print(-1)
            return
        cum += a
        source_sum += x * a

    # cum should be N
    ans = N * (N + 1) // 2 - source_sum
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Wait, if sum(A)==N, after loop cum=N. But what if pairs empty? M>=1. Good.
But note: We compute source_sum only after feasibility check; if impossible, return. Good.

Could use `it = iter(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))` to reduce memory. Let's decide final code. Simplicity vs memory. AtCoder Python memory likely okay. But to be safe, we can write a memory-friendly version:
import sys
def main():
    it = iter(sys.stdin.buffer.read().split())
    try:
        N = int(next(it))
    except StopIteration:
        return
    M = int(next(it))
    X = [0]*M
    A = [0]*M
    for i in range(M):
        X[i] = int(next(it))
    for i in range(M):
        A[i] = int(next(it))
    ...
This still stores X and A lists but not data int list. It creates bytes list from split? `iter(sys.stdin.buffer.read().split())` creates list of bytes, yes. But not int list. The bytes list persists as iterator over list? Actually `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` returns a list, and `iter()` returns list_iterator holding reference to that list. So bytes tokens remain in memory until iterator exhausted? After we consume all, the iterator still references list? It may hold list until iterator is garbage collected, but we don't keep it after reading? The variable `it` remains until end, holding list of bytes, which could be memory. We can delete `it` after reading. But simple `data` list of ints also memory. Both fine. If we want to avoid bytes list, we can use `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` unavoidable if using split. Could implement fast int parser, but overkill. The simple code is acceptable.

Maybe use:
data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
...
This is concise. I'll use that. It creates int list of all tokens, then slices X and A. Memory maybe ~400k ints ~11MB, plus slices ~3MB, plus bytes tokens during conversion freed. Fine.

But if we use `data` and then `X = data[2:2+M]`, `A = data[2+M:2+2*M]`, the original `data` list remains, duplicating references. We could avoid keeping `data` by deleting it after slicing:
X = data[2:2+M]
A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
del data
This frees the original list of references (not int objects, because X/A hold references to ints). But int objects for X/A remain; int objects for N/M? N/M are ints. The original data list contained references to all int objects; slicing copies references, so int objects are shared. Deleting data removes the list but not ints referenced by X/A. Good. We can `del data` to reduce memory. But not necessary. Could also parse with iterator to avoid data list. Let's write clean code:
def main():
    import sys
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    N, M = data[0], data[1]
    X = data[2:2+M]
    A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
    if sum(A) != N:
        print(-1)
        return
    pairs = sorted(zip(X, A))
    cum = 0
    source_sum = 0
    for x, a in pairs:
        if cum < x - 1:
            print(-1)
            return
        cum += a
        source_sum += x * a
    ans = N * (N + 1) // 2 - source_sum
    print(ans)
This is fine. Could delete data after slicing to reduce memory:
    del data
But if we delete data, X/A still hold ints. Good. Let's include `del data` maybe.
However, `N` and `M` are ints from data; if we delete data, N/M remain. Good.
Let's include `del data` after slicing? It is safe. But if we use `data` in list comprehension? no. We'll include:
    X = data[2:2+M]
    A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
    del data
But note `N` and `M` are assigned from data before del; okay.
This reduces memory a bit. But code clarity okay.

Alternatively, use iterator:
tokens = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
N = int(tokens[0]); M = int(tokens[1])
X = [int(t) for t in tokens[2:2+M]]
A = [int(t) for t in tokens[2+M:2+2*M]]
This creates bytes list and int lists. Similar. I think data list is fine.

Could use `it = iter(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))` to reduce memory. Let's decide final code. Simplicity vs memory. AtCoder Python memory likely okay. But to be safe, we can write a memory-friendly version:
import sys
def main():
    it = iter(sys.stdin.buffer.read().split())
    try:
        N = int(next(it))
    except StopIteration:
        return
    M = int(next(it))
    X = [0]*M
    A = [0]*M
    for i in range(M):
        X[i] = int(next(it))
    for i in range(M):
        A[i] = int(next(it))
    ...
This still stores X and A lists but not data int list. It creates bytes list from split? `iter(sys.stdin.buffer.read().split())` creates list of bytes, yes. But not int list. The bytes list persists as iterator over list? Actually `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` returns a list, and `iter()` returns list_iterator holding reference to that list. So bytes tokens remain in memory until iterator exhausted? After we consume all, the iterator still references list? It may hold list until iterator is garbage collected, but we don't keep it after reading? The variable `it` remains until end, holding list of bytes, which could be memory. We can delete `it` after reading. But simple `data` list of ints also memory. Both fine. If we want to avoid bytes list, we can use `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` unavoidable if using split. Could implement fast int parser, but overkill. The simple code is acceptable.

Maybe use:
data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
...
This is concise. I'll use that. It creates int list of all tokens, then slices X and A. Memory maybe ~400k ints ~11MB, plus slices ~3MB, plus bytes tokens during conversion freed. Fine.

But if we use `data` and then `X = data[2:2+M]`, `A = data[2+M:2+2*M]`, the original `data` list remains, duplicating references. We could avoid keeping `data` by deleting it after slicing:
X = data[2:2+M]
A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
del data
This frees the original list of references (not int objects, because X/A hold references to ints). But int objects for X/A remain; int objects for N/M? N/M are ints. The original data list contained references to all int objects; slicing copies references, so int objects are shared. Deleting data removes the list but not ints referenced by X/A. Good. We can `del data` to reduce memory. But not necessary. Could also parse with iterator to avoid data list. Let's write clean code:
def main():
    import sys
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    N, M = data[0], data[1]
    X = data[2:2+M]
    A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
    if sum(A) != N:
        print(-1)
        return
    pairs = sorted(zip(X, A))
    cum = 0
    source_sum = 0
    for x, a in pairs:
        if cum < x - 1:
            print(-1)
            return
        cum += a
        source_sum += x * a
    ans = N * (N + 1) // 2 - source_sum
    print(ans)
This is fine. Could delete data after slicing to reduce memory:
    del data
But if we delete data, X/A still hold ints. Good. Let's include `del data` maybe.
However, `N` and `M` are ints from data; if we delete data, N/M remain. Good.
Let's include `del data` after slicing? It is safe. But if we use `data` in list comprehension? no. We'll include:
    X = data[2:2+M]
    A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
    del data
But note `N` and `M` are assigned from data before del; okay.
This reduces memory a bit. But code clarity okay.

Alternatively, use iterator:
tokens = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
N = int(tokens[0]); M = int(tokens[1])
X = [int(t) for t in tokens[2:2+M]]
A = [int(t) for t in tokens[2+M:2+2*M]]
This creates bytes list and int lists. Similar. I think data list is fine.

def main():
    import sys
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    N, M = data[0], data[1]
    X = data[2:2+M]
    A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
    if sum(A) != N:
        print(-1)
        return
    pairs = sorted(zip(X, A))
    cum = 0
    source_sum = 0

Could use `it = iter(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))` to reduce memory. Let's decide final code. Simplicity vs memory. AtCoder Python memory likely okay. But to be safe, we can write a memory-friendly version:
import sys
def main():
    it = iter(sys.stdin.buffer.read().split())
    try:
        N = int(next(it))
    except StopIteration:
        return
    M = int(next(it))
    X = [0]*M
    A = [0]*M
    for i in range(M):
        X[i] = int(next(it))
    for i in range(M):
        A[i] = int(next(it))
    ...
This still stores X and A lists but not data int list. It creates bytes list from split? `iter(sys.stdin.buffer.read().split())` creates list of bytes, yes. But not int list. The bytes list persists as iterator over list? Actually `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` returns a list, and `iter()` returns list_iterator holding reference to that list. So bytes tokens remain in memory until iterator exhausted? After we consume all, the iterator still references list? It may hold list until iterator is garbage collected, but we don't keep it after reading? The variable `it` remains until end, holding list of bytes, which could be memory. We can delete `it` after reading. But simple `data` list of ints also memory. Both fine. If we want to avoid bytes list, we can use `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` unavoidable if using split. Could implement fast int parser, but overkill. The simple code is acceptable.

data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))

This is concise. I'll use that. It creates int list of all tokens, then slices X and A. Memory maybe ~400k ints ~11MB, plus slices ~3MB, plus bytes tokens during conversion freed. Fine.

But if we use `data` and then `X = data[2:2+M]`, `A = data[2+M:2+2*M]`, the original `data` list remains, duplicating references. We could avoid keeping `data` by deleting it after slicing:
X = data[2:2+M]
A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
del data
This frees the original list of references (not int objects, because X/A hold references to ints). But int objects for X/A remain; int objects for N/M? N/M are ints. The original data list contained references to all int objects; slicing copies references, so int objects are shared. Deleting data removes the list but not ints referenced by X/A. Good. We can `del data` to reduce memory. But not necessary. Could also parse with iterator to avoid data list. Let's write clean code:
def main():
    import sys
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    N, M = data[0], data[1]
    X = data[2:2+M]
    A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
    if sum(A) != N:
        print(-1)
        return
    pairs = sorted(zip(X, A))
    cum = 0
    source_sum = 0
    for x, a in pairs:
        if cum < x - 1:
            print(-1)
            return
        cum += a
        source_sum += x * a
    ans = N * (N + 1) // 2 - source_sum
    print(ans)

    del data

    del data

Could use `it = iter(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))` to reduce memory. Let's decide final code. Simplicity vs memory. AtCoder Python memory likely okay. But to be safe, we can write a memory-friendly version:
import sys
def main():
    it = iter(sys.stdin.buffer.read().split())
    try:
        N = int(next(it))
    except StopIteration:
        return
    M = int(next(it))
    X = [0]*M
    A = [0]*M
    for i in range(M):
        X[i] = int(next(it))
    for i in range(M):
        A[i] = int(next(it))
    ...
This still stores X and A lists but not data int list. It creates bytes list from split? `iter(sys.stdin.buffer.read().split())` creates list of bytes, yes. But not int list. The bytes list persists as iterator over list? Actually `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` returns a list, and `iter()` returns list_iterator holding reference to that list. So bytes tokens remain in memory until iterator exhausted? After we consume all, the iterator still references list? It may hold list until iterator is garbage collected, but we don't keep it after reading? The variable `it` remains until end, holding list of bytes, which could be memory. We can delete `it` after reading. But simple `data` list of ints also memory. Both fine. If we want to avoid bytes list, we can use `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` unavoidable if using split. Could implement fast int parser, but overkill. The simple code is acceptable.

data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
...
This is concise. I'll use that. It creates int list of all tokens, then slices X and A. Memory maybe ~400k ints ~11MB, plus slices ~3MB, plus bytes tokens during conversion freed. Fine.

But if we use `data` and then `X = data[2:2+M]`, `A = data[2+M:2+2*M]`, the original `data` list remains, duplicating references. We could avoid keeping `data` by deleting it after slicing:
X = data[2:2+M]
A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
del data
This frees the original list of references (not int objects, because X/A hold references to ints). But int objects for X/A remain; int objects for N/M? N/M are ints. The original data list contained references to all int objects; slicing copies references, so int objects are shared. Deleting data removes the list but not ints referenced by X/A. Good. We can `del data` to reduce memory. But not necessary. Could also parse with iterator to avoid data list. Let's write clean code:
def main():
    import sys
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    N, M = data[0], data[1]
    X = data[2:2+M]
    A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
    if sum(A) != N:
        print(-1)
        return
    pairs = sorted(zip(X, A))
    cum = 0
    source_sum = 0
    for x, a in pairs:
        if cum < x - 1:
            print(-1)
            return
        cum += a
        source_sum += x * a
    ans = N * (N + 1) // 2 - source_sum
    print(ans)
This is fine. Could delete data after slicing to reduce memory:
    del data
But if we delete data, X/A still hold ints. Good. Let's include `del data` maybe.
However, `N` and `M` are ints from data; if we delete data, N/M remain. Good.
Let's include `del data` after slicing? It is safe. But if we use `data` in list comprehension? no. We'll include:
    X = data[2:2+M]
    A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
    del data
But note `N` and `M` are assigned from data before del; okay.
This reduces memory a bit. But code clarity okay.

Alternatively, use iterator:
tokens = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
N = int(tokens[0]); M = int(tokens[1])
X = [int(t) for t in tokens[2:2+M]]
A = [int(t) for t in tokens[2+M:2+2*M]]
This creates bytes list and int lists. Similar. I think data list is fine.

Greedy: cell1 leave1 move3 to2; cell2 leave1 move2 to3; cell3 leave1 move1 to4; cell4 initial1+incoming1=2 leave1 move1 to5. Final. Ops3+2+1+1=7. Formula total15 - source_sum(4+4=8)=7. Feasible. Could use `it = iter(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))` to reduce memory. Let's decide final code. Simplicity vs memory. AtCoder Python memory likely okay. But to be safe, we can write a memory-friendly version:
import sys
def main():
    it = iter(sys.stdin.buffer.read().split())
    try:
        N = int(next(it))
    except StopIteration:
        return
    M = int(next(it))
    X = [0]*M
    A = [0]*M
    for i in range(M):
        X[i] = int(next(it))
    for i in range(M):
        A[i] = int(next(it))
    ...
This still stores X and A lists but not data int list. It creates bytes list from split? `iter(sys.stdin.buffer.read().split())` creates list of bytes, yes. But not int list. The bytes list persists as iterator over list? Actually `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` returns a list, and `iter()` returns list_iterator holding reference to that list. So bytes tokens remain in memory until iterator exhausted? After we consume all, the iterator still references list? It may hold list until iterator is garbage collected, but we don't keep it after reading? The variable `it` remains until end, holding list of bytes, which could be memory. We can delete `it` after reading. But simple `data` list of ints also memory. Both fine. If we want to avoid bytes list, we can use `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` unavoidable if using split. Could implement fast int parser, but overkill. The simple code is acceptable.

Maybe use:
data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
...
This is concise. I'll use that. It creates int list of all tokens, then slices X and A. Memory maybe ~400k ints ~11MB, plus slices ~3MB, plus bytes tokens during conversion freed. Fine.

But if we use `data` and then `X = data[2:2+M]`, `A = data[2+M:2+2*M]`, the original `data` list remains, duplicating references. We could avoid keeping `data` by deleting it after slicing:
X = data[2:2+M]
A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
del data
This frees the original list of references (not int objects, because X/A hold references to ints). But int objects for X/A remain; int objects for N/M? N/M are ints. The original data list contained references to all int objects; slicing copies references, so int objects are shared. Deleting data removes the list but not ints referenced by X/A. Good. We can `del data` to reduce memory. But not necessary. Could also parse with iterator to avoid data list. Let's write clean code:
def main():
    import sys
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    N, M = data[0], data[1]
    X = data[2:2+M]
    A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
    if sum(A) != N:
        print(-1)
        return
    pairs = sorted(zip(X, A))
    cum = 0
    source_sum = 0
    for x, a in pairs:
        if cum < x - 1:
            print(-1)
            return
        cum += a
        source_sum += x * a
    ans = N * (N + 1) // 2 - source_sum
    print(ans)
This is fine. Could delete data after slicing to reduce memory:
    del data
But if we delete data, X/A still hold ints. Good. Let's include `del data` maybe.
However, `N` and `M` are ints from data; if we delete data, N/M remain. Good.
Let's include `del data` after slicing? It is safe. But if we use `data` in list comprehension? no. We'll include:
    X = data[2:2+M]
    A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
    del data
But note `N` and `M` are assigned from data before del; okay.
This reduces memory a bit. But code clarity okay.

Alternatively, use iterator:
tokens = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
N = int(tokens[0]); M = int(tokens[1])
X = [int(t) for t in tokens[2:2+M]]
A = [int(t) for t in tokens[2+M:2+2*M]]
This creates bytes list and int lists. Similar. I think data list is fine.

    if not data:

Could use `it = iter(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))` to reduce memory. Let's decide final code. Simplicity vs memory. AtCoder Python memory likely okay. But to be safe, we can write a memory-friendly version:
import sys
def main():
    it = iter(sys.stdin.buffer.read().split())
    try:
        N = int(next(it))
    except StopIteration:
        return
    M = int(next(it))
    X = [0]*M
    A = [0]*M
    for i in range(M):
        X[i] = int(next(it))
    for i in range(M):
        A[i] = int(next(it))
    ...
This still stores X and A lists but not data int list. It creates bytes list from split? `iter(sys.stdin.buffer.read().split())` creates list of bytes, yes. But not int list. The bytes list persists as iterator over list? Actually `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` returns a list, and `iter()` returns list_iterator holding reference to that list. So bytes tokens remain in memory until iterator exhausted? After we consume all, the iterator still references list? It may hold list until iterator is garbage collected, but we don't keep it after reading? The variable `it` remains until end, holding list of bytes, which could be memory. We can delete `it` after reading. But simple `data` list of ints also memory. Both fine. If we want to avoid bytes list, we can use `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` unavoidable if using split. Could implement fast int parser, but overkill. The simple code is acceptable.

Maybe use:
data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
...
This is concise. I'll use that. It creates int list of all tokens, then slices X and A. Memory maybe ~400k ints ~11MB, plus slices ~3MB, plus bytes tokens during conversion freed. Fine.

But if we use `data` and then `X = data[2:2+M]`, `A = data[2+M:2+2*M]`, the original `data` list remains, duplicating references. We could avoid keeping `data` by deleting it after slicing:
X = data[2:2+M]
A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
del data
This frees the original list of references (not int objects, because X/A hold references to ints). But int objects for X/A remain; int objects for N/M? N/M are ints. The original data list contained references to all int objects; slicing copies references, so int objects are shared. Deleting data removes the list but not ints referenced by X/A. Good. We can `del data` to reduce memory. But not necessary. Could also parse with iterator to avoid data list. Let's write clean code:
def main():
    import sys
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    N, M = data[0], data[1]
    X = data[2:2+M]
    A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
    if sum(A) != N:
        print(-1)
        return
    pairs = sorted(zip(X, A))
    cum = 0
    source_sum = 0
    for x, a in pairs:
        if cum < x - 1:
            print(-1)
            return
        cum += a
        source_sum += x * a
    ans = N * (N + 1) // 2 - source_sum
    print(ans)
This is fine. Could delete data after slicing to reduce memory:
    del data
But if we delete data, X/A still hold ints. Good. Let's include `del data` maybe.
However, `N` and `M` are ints from data; if we delete data, N/M remain. Good.
Let's include `del data` after slicing? It is safe. But if we use `data` in list comprehension? no. We'll include:
    X = data[2:2+M]
    A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
    del data
But note `N` and `M` are assigned from data before del; okay.
This reduces memory a bit. But code clarity okay.

Alternatively, use iterator:
tokens = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
N = int(tokens[0]); M = int(tokens[1])
X = [int(t) for t in tokens[2:2+M]]
A = [int(t) for t in tokens[2+M:2+2*M]]
This creates bytes list and int lists. Similar. I think data list is fine.

We can `del data` to reduce memory. But not necessary. Could also parse with iterator to avoid data list. Let's write clean code:
def main():
    import sys
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    N, M = data[0], data[1]
    X = data[2:2+M]
    A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
    if sum(A) != N:
        print(-1)
        return
    pairs = sorted(zip(X, A))
    cum = 0
    source_sum = 0
    for x, a in pairs:
        if cum < x - 1:
            print(-1)
            return
        cum += a
        source_sum += x * a
    ans = N * (N + 1) // 2 - source_sum
    print(ans)
This is fine. Could delete data after slicing to reduce memory:
    del data
But if we delete data, X/A still hold ints. Good. Let's include `del data` maybe.
However, `N` and `M` are ints from data; if we delete data, N/M remain. Good.
Let's include `del data` after slicing? It is safe. But if we use `data` in list comprehension? no. We'll include:
    X = data[2:2+M]
    A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
    del data
But note `N` and `M` are assigned from data before del; okay.
This reduces memory a bit. But code clarity okay.

But to be safe, we can write a memory-friendly version:
import sys
def main():
    it = iter(sys.stdin.buffer.read().split())
    try:
        N = int(next(it))
    except StopIteration:
        return
    M = int(next(it))
    X = [0]*M
    A = [0]*M
    for i in range(M):
        X[i] = int(next(it))
    for i in range(M):
        A[i] = int(next(it))
    ...

The bytes list persists as iterator over list? Actually `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` returns a list, and `iter()` returns list_iterator holding reference to that list. So bytes tokens remain in memory until iterator exhausted? After we consume all, the iterator still references list? It may hold list until iterator is garbage collected, but we don't keep it after reading? The variable `it` remains until end, holding list of bytes, which could be memory. We can delete `it` after reading. But simple `data` list of ints also memory. Both fine. If we want to avoid bytes list, we can use `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` unavoidable if using split. Could use `it = iter(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))` to reduce memory. Let's decide final code. Simplicity vs memory. AtCoder Python memory likely okay. But to be safe, we can write a memory-friendly version:
import sys
def main():
    it = iter(sys.stdin.buffer.read().split())
    try:
        N = int(next(it))
    except StopIteration:
        return
    M = int(next(it))
    X = [0]*M
    A = [0]*M
    for i in range(M):
        X[i] = int(next(it))
    for i in range(M):
        A[i] = int(next(it))
    ...
This still stores X and A lists but not data int list. It creates bytes list from split? `iter(sys.stdin.buffer.read().split())` creates list of bytes, yes. But not int list. The bytes list persists as iterator over list? Actually `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` returns a list, and `iter()` returns list_iterator holding reference to that list. So bytes tokens remain in memory until iterator exhausted? After we consume all, the iterator still references list? It may hold list until iterator is garbage collected, but we don't keep it after reading? The variable `it` remains until end, holding list of bytes, which could be memory. We can delete `it` after reading. But simple `data` list of ints also memory. Both fine. If we want to avoid bytes list, we can use `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` unavoidable if using split. Could implement fast int parser, but overkill. The simple code is acceptable.

data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
...
This is concise. I'll use that. It creates int list of all tokens, then slices X and A. Memory maybe ~400k ints ~11MB, plus slices ~3MB, plus bytes tokens during conversion freed. Fine.

But if we use `data` and then `X = data[2:2+M]`, `A = data[2+M:2+2*M]`, the original `data` list remains, duplicating references. We could avoid keeping `data` by deleting it after slicing:
X = data[2:2+M]
A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
del data
This frees the original list of references (not int objects, because X/A hold references to ints). But int objects for X/A remain; int objects for N/M? N/M are ints. The original data list contained references to all int objects; slicing copies references, so int objects are shared. Deleting data removes the list but not ints referenced by X/A. Good. We can `del data` to reduce memory. But not necessary. Could also parse with iterator to avoid data list. Let's write clean code:
def main():
    import sys
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    N, M = data[0], data[1]
    X = data[2:2+M]
    A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
    if sum(A) != N:
        print(-1)
        return
    pairs = sorted(zip(X, A))
    cum = 0
    source_sum = 0
    for x, a in pairs:
        if cum < x - 1:
            print(-1)
            return
        cum += a
        source_sum += x * a
    ans = N * (N + 1) // 2 - source_sum
    print(ans)
This is fine. Could delete data after slicing to reduce memory:
    del data
But if we delete data, X/A still hold ints. Good. Let's include `del data` maybe.
However, `N` and `M` are ints from data; if we delete data, N/M remain. Good.
Let's include `del data` after slicing? It is safe. But if we use `data` in list comprehension? no. We'll include:
    X = data[2:2+M]
    A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
    del data
But note `N` and `M` are assigned from data before del; okay.
This reduces memory a bit. But code clarity okay.

Alternatively, use iterator:
tokens = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
N = int(tokens[0]); M = int(tokens[1])
X = [int(t) for t in tokens[2:2+M]]
A = [int(t) for t in tokens[2+M:2+2*M]]
This creates bytes list and int lists. Similar. I think data list is fine.

Could use `it = iter(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))` to reduce memory. Let's decide final code. Simplicity vs memory. AtCoder Python memory likely okay. But to be safe, we can write a memory-friendly version:
import sys
def main():
    it = iter(sys.stdin.buffer.read().split())
    try:
        N = int(next(it))
    except StopIteration:
        return
    M = int(next(it))
    X = [0]*M
    A = [0]*M
    for i in range(M):
        X[i] = int(next(it))
    for i in range(M):
        A[i] = int(next(it))
    ...
This still stores X and A lists but not data int list. It creates bytes list from split? `iter(sys.stdin.buffer.read().split())` creates list of bytes, yes. But not int list. The bytes list persists as iterator over list? Actually `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` returns a list, and `iter()` returns list_iterator holding reference to that list. So bytes tokens remain in memory until iterator exhausted? After we consume all, the iterator still references list? It may hold list until iterator is garbage collected, but we don't keep it after reading? The variable `it` remains until end, holding list of bytes, which could be memory. We can delete `it` after reading. But simple `data` list of ints also memory. Both fine. If we want to avoid bytes list, we can use `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` unavoidable if using split. Could implement fast int parser, but overkill. The simple code is acceptable.

Maybe use:
data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
...
This is concise. I'll use that. It creates int list of all tokens, then slices X and A. Memory maybe ~400k ints ~11MB, plus slices ~3MB, plus bytes tokens during conversion freed. Fine.

But if we use `data` and then `X = data[2:2+M]`, `A = data[2+M:2+2*M]`, the original `data` list remains, duplicating references. We could avoid keeping `data` by deleting it after slicing:
X = data[2:2+M]
A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
del data
This frees the original list of references (not int objects, because X/A hold references to ints). But int objects for X/A remain; int objects for N/M? N/M are ints. The original data list contained references to all int objects; slicing copies references, so int objects are shared. Deleting data removes the list but not ints referenced by X/A. Good. We can `del data` to reduce memory. But not necessary. Could also parse with iterator to avoid data list. Let's write clean code:
def main():
    import sys
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    N, M = data[0], data[1]
    X = data[2:2+M]
    A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
    if sum(A) != N:
        print(-1)
        return
    pairs = sorted(zip(X, A))
    cum = 0
    source_sum = 0
    for x, a in pairs:
        if cum < x - 1:
            print(-1)
            return
        cum += a
        source_sum += x * a
    ans = N * (N + 1) // 2 - source_sum
    print(ans)
This is fine. Could delete data after slicing to reduce memory:
    del data
But if we delete data, X/A still hold ints. Good. Let's include `del data` maybe.
However, `N` and `M` are ints from data; if we delete data, N/M remain. Good.
Let's include `del data` after slicing? It is safe. But if we use `data` in list comprehension? no. We'll include:
    X = data[2:2+M]
    A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
    del data
But note `N` and `M` are assigned from data before del; okay.
This reduces memory a bit. But code clarity okay.

Alternatively, use iterator:
tokens = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
N = int(tokens[0]); M = int(tokens[1])
X = [int(t) for t in tokens[2:2+M]]
A = [int(t) for t in tokens[2+M:2+2*M]]
This creates bytes list and int lists. Similar. I think data list is fine.

Could use `it = iter(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))` to reduce memory. Let's decide final code. Simplicity vs memory. AtCoder Python memory likely okay. But to be safe, we can write a memory-friendly version:
import sys
def main():
    it = iter(sys.stdin.buffer.read().split())
    try:
        N = int(next(it))
    except StopIteration:
        return
    M = int(next(it))
    X = [0]*M
    A = [0]*M
    for i in range(M):
        X[i] = int(next(it))
    for i in range(M):
        A[i] = int(next(it))
    ...
This still stores X and A lists but not data int list. It creates bytes list from split? `iter(sys.stdin.buffer.read().split())` creates list of bytes, yes. But not int list. The bytes list persists as iterator over list? Actually `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` returns a list, and `iter()` returns list_iterator holding reference to that list. So bytes tokens remain in memory until iterator exhausted? After we consume all, the iterator still references list? It may hold list until iterator is garbage collected, but we don't keep it after reading? The variable `it` remains until end, holding list of bytes, which could be memory. We can delete `it` after reading. But simple `data` list of ints also memory. Both fine. If we want to avoid bytes list, we can use `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` unavoidable if using split. Could implement fast int parser, but overkill. The simple code is acceptable.

Maybe use:
data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
...
This is concise. I'll use that. It creates int list of all tokens, then slices X and A. Memory maybe ~400k ints ~11MB, plus slices ~3MB, plus bytes tokens during conversion freed. Fine.

But if we use `data` and then `X = data[2:2+M]`, `A = data[2+M:2+2*M]`, the original `data` list remains, duplicating references. We could avoid keeping `data` by deleting it after slicing:
X = data[2:2+M]
A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
del data
This frees the original list of references (not int objects, because X/A hold references to ints). But int objects for X/A remain; int objects for N/M? N/M are ints. The original data list contained references to all int objects; slicing copies references, so int objects are shared. Deleting data removes the list but not ints referenced by X/A. Good. We can `del data` to reduce memory. But not necessary. Could also parse with iterator to avoid data list. Let's write clean code:
def main():
    import sys
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    N, M = data[0], data[1]
    X = data[2:2+M]
    A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
    if sum(A) != N:
        print(-1)
        return
    pairs = sorted(zip(X, A))
    cum = 0
    source_sum = 0
    for x, a in pairs:
        if cum < x - 1:
            print(-1)
            return
        cum += a
        source_sum += x * a
    ans = N * (N + 1) // 2 - source_sum
    print(ans)
This is fine. Could delete data after slicing to reduce memory:
    del data
But if we delete data, X/A still hold ints. Good. Let's include `del data` maybe.
However, `N` and `M` are ints from data; if we delete data, N/M remain. Good.
Let's include `del data` after slicing? It is safe. But if we use `data` in list comprehension? no. We'll include:
    X = data[2:2+M]
    A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
    del data
But note `N` and `M` are assigned from data before del; okay.
This reduces memory a bit. But code clarity okay.

Alternatively, use iterator:
tokens = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
N = int(tokens[0]); M = int(tokens[1])
X = [int(t) for t in tokens[2:2+M]]
A = [int(t) for t in tokens[2+M:2+2*M]]
This creates bytes list and int lists. Similar. I think data list is fine.

But if we use `data` and then `X = data[2:2+M]`, `A = data[2+M:2+2*M]`, the original `data` list remains, duplicating references. We could avoid keeping `data` by deleting it after slicing:

A = data[2+M:2+2*M]

This frees the original list of references (not int objects, because X/A hold references to ints). But int objects for X/A remain; int objects for N/M? N/M are ints. The original data list contained references to all int objects; slicing copies references, so int objects are shared. Deleting data removes the list but not ints referenced by X/A. Good. We can `del data` to reduce memory. But not necessary. Could also parse with iterator to avoid data list. Let's write clean code:
def main():
    import sys
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    N, M = data[0], data[1]
    X = data[2:2+M]
    A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
    if sum(A) != N:
        print(-1)
        return
    pairs = sorted(zip(X, A))
    cum = 0
    source_sum = 0
    for x, a in pairs:
        if cum < x - 1:
            print(-1)
            return
        cum += a
        source_sum += x * a
    ans = N * (N + 1) // 2 - source_sum
    print(ans)
This is fine. Could delete data after slicing to reduce memory:
    del data
But if we delete data, X/A still hold ints. Good. Let's include `del data` maybe.
However, `N` and `M` are ints from data; if we delete data, N/M remain. Good.
Let's include `del data` after slicing? It is safe. But if we use `data` in list comprehension? no. We'll include:
    X = data[2:2+M]
    A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
    del data
But note `N` and `M` are assigned from data before del; okay.
This reduces memory a bit. But code clarity okay.

But to be safe, we can write a memory-friendly version:
import sys
def main():
    it = iter(sys.stdin.buffer.read().split())
    try:
        N = int(next(it))
    except StopIteration:
        return
    M = int(next(it))
    X = [0]*M
    A = [0]*M
    for i in range(M):
        X[i] = int(next(it))
    for i in range(M):
        A[i] = int(next(it))
    ...

The bytes list persists as iterator over list? Actually `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` returns a list, and `iter()` returns list_iterator holding reference to that list. So bytes tokens remain in memory until iterator exhausted? After we consume all, the iterator still references list? It may hold list until iterator is garbage collected, but we don't keep it after reading? The variable `it` remains until end, holding list of bytes, which could be memory. We can delete `it` after reading. But simple `data` list of ints also memory. Both fine. If we want to avoid bytes list, we can use `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` unavoidable if using split. Could use `it = iter(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))` to reduce memory. Let's decide final code. Simplicity vs memory. AtCoder Python memory likely okay. But to be safe, we can write a memory-friendly version:
import sys
def main():
    it = iter(sys.stdin.buffer.read().split())
    try:
        N = int(next(it))
    except StopIteration:
        return
    M = int(next(it))
    X = [0]*M
    A = [0]*M
    for i in range(M):
        X[i] = int(next(it))
    for i in range(M):
        A[i] = int(next(it))
    ...
This still stores X and A lists but not data int list. It creates bytes list from split? `iter(sys.stdin.buffer.read().split())` creates list of bytes, yes. But not int list. The bytes list persists as iterator over list? Actually `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` returns a list, and `iter()` returns list_iterator holding reference to that list. So bytes tokens remain in memory until iterator exhausted? After we consume all, the iterator still references list? It may hold list until iterator is garbage collected, but we don't keep it after reading? The variable `it` remains until end, holding list of bytes, which could be memory. We can delete `it` after reading. But simple `data` list of ints also memory. Both fine. If we want to avoid bytes list, we can use `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` unavoidable if using split. Could implement fast int parser, but overkill. The simple code is acceptable.

Maybe use:
data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
...
This is concise. I'll use that. It creates int list of all tokens, then slices X and A. Memory maybe ~400k ints ~11MB, plus slices ~3MB, plus bytes tokens during conversion freed. Fine.

But if we use `data` and then `X = data[2:2+M]`, `A = data[2+M:2+2*M]`, the original `data` list remains, duplicating references. We could avoid keeping `data` by deleting it after slicing:
X = data[2:2+M]
A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
del data
This frees the original list of references (not int objects, because X/A hold references to ints). But int objects for X/A remain; int objects for N/M? N/M are ints. The original data list contained references to all int objects; slicing copies references, so int objects are shared. Deleting data removes the list but not ints referenced by X/A. Good. We can `del data` to reduce memory. But not necessary. Could also parse with iterator to avoid data list. Let's write clean code:
def main():
    import sys
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    N, M = data[0], data[1]
    X = data[2:2+M]
    A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
    if sum(A) != N:
        print(-1)
        return
    pairs = sorted(zip(X, A))
    cum = 0
    source_sum = 0
    for x, a in pairs:
        if cum < x - 1:
            print(-1)
            return
        cum += a
        source_sum += x * a
    ans = N * (N + 1) // 2 - source_sum
    print(ans)
This is fine. Could delete data after slicing to reduce memory:
    del data
But if we delete data, X/A still hold ints. Good. Let's include `del data` maybe.
However, `N` and `M` are ints from data; if we delete data, N/M remain. Good.
Let's include `del data` after slicing? It is safe. But if we use `data` in list comprehension? no. We'll include:
    X = data[2:2+M]
    A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
    del data
But note `N` and `M` are assigned from data before del; okay.
This reduces memory a bit. But code clarity okay.

Alternatively, use iterator:
tokens = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
N = int(tokens[0]); M = int(tokens[1])
X = [int(t) for t in tokens[2:2+M]]
A = [int(t) for t in tokens[2+M:2+2*M]]
This creates bytes list and int lists. Similar. I think data list is fine.

Could use `it = iter(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))` to reduce memory. Let's decide final code. Simplicity vs memory. AtCoder Python memory likely okay. But to be safe, we can write a memory-friendly version:
import sys
def main():
    it = iter(sys.stdin.buffer.read().split())
    try:
        N = int(next(it))
    except StopIteration:
        return
    M = int(next(it))
    X = [0]*M
    A = [0]*M
    for i in range(M):
        X[i] = int(next(it))
    for i in range(M):
        A[i] = int(next(it))
    ...
This still stores X and A lists but not data int list. It creates bytes list from split? `iter(sys.stdin.buffer.read().split())` creates list of bytes, yes. But not int list. The bytes list persists as iterator over list? Actually `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` returns a list, and `iter()` returns list_iterator holding reference to that list. So bytes tokens remain in memory until iterator exhausted? After we consume all, the iterator still references list? It may hold list until iterator is garbage collected, but we don't keep it after reading? The variable `it` remains until end, holding list of bytes, which could be memory. We can delete `it` after reading. But simple `data` list of ints also memory. Both fine. If we want to avoid bytes list, we can use `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` unavoidable if using split. Could implement fast int parser, but overkill. The simple code is acceptable.

Maybe use:
data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
...
This is concise. I'll use that. It creates int list of all tokens, then slices X and A. Memory maybe ~400k ints ~11MB, plus slices ~3MB, plus bytes tokens during conversion freed. Fine.

But if we use `data` and then `X = data[2:2+M]`, `A = data[2+M:2+2*M]`, the original `data` list remains, duplicating references. We could avoid keeping `data` by deleting it after slicing:
X = data[2:2+M]
A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
del data
This frees the original list of references (not int objects, because X/A hold references to ints). But int objects for X/A remain; int objects for N/M? N/M are ints. The original data list contained references to all int objects; slicing copies references, so int objects are shared. Deleting data removes the list but not ints referenced by X/A. Good. We can `del data` to reduce memory. But not necessary. Could also parse with iterator to avoid data list. Let's write clean code:
def main():
    import sys
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    N, M = data[0], data[1]
    X = data[2:2+M]
    A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
    if sum(A) != N:
        print(-1)
        return
    pairs = sorted(zip(X, A))
    cum = 0
    source_sum = 0
    for x, a in pairs:
        if cum < x - 1:
            print(-1)
            return
        cum += a
        source_sum += x * a
    ans = N * (N + 1) // 2 - source_sum
    print(ans)
This is fine. Could delete data after slicing to reduce memory:
    del data
But if we delete data, X/A still hold ints. Good. Let's include `del data` maybe.
However, `N` and `M` are ints from data; if we delete data, N/M remain. Good.
Let's include `del data` after slicing? It is safe. But if we use `data` in list comprehension? no. We'll include:
    X = data[2:2+M]
    A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
    del data
But note `N` and `M` are assigned from data before del; okay.
This reduces memory a bit. But code clarity okay.

Alternatively, use iterator:
tokens = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
N = int(tokens[0]); M = int(tokens[1])
X = [int(t) for t in tokens[2:2+M]]
A = [int(t) for t in tokens[2+M:2+2*M]]
This creates bytes list and int lists. Similar. I think data list is fine.

Could use `it = iter(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))` to reduce memory. Let's decide final code. Simplicity vs memory. AtCoder Python memory likely okay. But to be safe, we can write a memory-friendly version:
import sys
def main():
    it = iter(sys.stdin.buffer.read().split())
    try:
        N = int(next(it))
    except StopIteration:
        return
    M = int(next(it))
    X = [0]*M
    A = [0]*M
    for i in range(M):
        X[i] = int(next(it))
    for i in range(M):
        A[i] = int(next(it))
    ...
This still stores X and A lists but not data int list. It creates bytes list from split? `iter(sys.stdin.buffer.read().split())` creates list of bytes, yes. But not int list. The bytes list persists as iterator over list? Actually `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` returns a list, and `iter()` returns list_iterator holding reference to that list. So bytes tokens remain in memory until iterator exhausted? After we consume all, the iterator still references list? It may hold list until iterator is garbage collected, but we don't keep it after reading? The variable `it` remains until end, holding list of bytes, which could be memory. We can delete `it` after reading. But simple `data` list of ints also memory. Both fine. If we want to avoid bytes list, we can use `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` unavoidable if using split. Could implement fast int parser, but overkill. The simple code is acceptable.

Maybe use:
data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
...
This is concise. I'll use that. It creates int list of all tokens, then slices X and A. Memory maybe ~400k ints ~11MB, plus slices ~3MB, plus bytes tokens during conversion freed. Fine.

But if we use `data` and then `X = data[2:2+M]`, `A = data[2+M:2+2*M]`, the original `data` list remains, duplicating references. We could avoid keeping `data` by deleting it after slicing:
X = data[2:2+M]
A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
del data
This frees the original list of references (not int objects, because X/A hold references to ints). But int objects for X/A remain; int objects for N/M? N/M are ints. The original data list contained references to all int objects; slicing copies references, so int objects are shared. Deleting data removes the list but not ints referenced by X/A. Good. We can `del data` to reduce memory. But not necessary. Could also parse with iterator to avoid data list. Let's write clean code:
def main():
    import sys
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    N, M = data[0], data[1]
    X = data[2:2+M]
    A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
    if sum(A) != N:
        print(-1)
        return
    pairs = sorted(zip(X, A))
    cum = 0
    source_sum = 0
    for x, a in pairs:
        if cum < x - 1:
            print(-1)
            return
        cum += a
        source_sum += x * a
    ans = N * (N + 1) // 2 - source_sum
    print(ans)
This is fine. Could delete data after slicing to reduce memory:
    del data
But if we delete data, X/A still hold ints. Good. Let's include `del data` maybe.
However, `N` and `M` are ints from data; if we delete data, N/M remain. Good.
Let's include `del data` after slicing? It is safe. But if we use `data` in list comprehension? no. We'll include:
    X = data[2:2+M]
    A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
    del data
But note `N` and `M` are assigned from data before del; okay.
This reduces memory a bit. But code clarity okay.

Alternatively, use iterator:
tokens = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
N = int(tokens[0]); M = int(tokens[1])
X = [int(t) for t in tokens[2:2+M]]
A = [int(t) for t in tokens[2+M:2+2*M]]
This creates bytes list and int lists. Similar. I think data list is fine.

def main():
    import sys
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    N, M = data[0], data[1]
    X = data[2:2+M]
    A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
    if sum(A) != N:
        print(-1)
        return
    pairs = sorted(zip(X, A))
    cum = 0
    source_sum = 0
    for x, a in pairs:
        if cum < x - 1:
            print(-1)
            return
        cum += a
        source_sum += x * a
    ans = N * (N + 1) // 2 - source_sum
    print(ans)
This is fine. Could delete data after slicing to reduce memory:
    del data
But if we delete data, X/A still hold ints. Good. Let's include `del data` maybe.
However, `N` and `M` are ints from data; if we delete data, N/M remain. Good.
Let's include `del data` after slicing? It is safe. But if we use `data` in list comprehension? no. We'll include:
    X = data[2:2+M]
    A = data[2+M:2+2*M]
    del data
But note `N` and `M` are assigned from data before del; okay.
This reduces memory a bit. But code clarity okay.

import sys
def main():
    it = iter(sys.stdin.buffer.read().split())
    try:
        N = int(next(it))
    except StopIteration:
        return
    M = int(next(it))
    X = [0]*M
    A = [0]*M
    for i in range(M):
        X[i] = int(next(it))
    for i in range(M):
        A[i] = int(next(it))
    ...

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return

    N, M = data[0], data[1]
    X = data[2:2 + M]
    A = data[2 + M:2 + 2 * M]

    if sum(A) != N:
        print(-1)
        return

    pairs = sorted(zip(X, A))

    cum = 0
    source_sum = 0

    for x, a in pairs:
        # Stones before x must be enough to fill cells 1..x-1.
        if cum < x - 1:
            print(-1)
            return
        cum += a
        source_sum += x * a

    ans = N * (N + 1) // 2 - source_sum
    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
